Heuristic Justification The following adapts the entropic derivation of the quantum Singleton bound by incorporating twisted sectors from Chen-Ruan cohomology (see [ 10 ] ), which add virtual degrees of freedom interpretable as entropy corrections. This argument relies on interpretive links from TQFT to quantum coding, providing motivation rather than a fully rigorous proof in coding theory terms. First, recall the standard Singleton bound d ≤ n − k + 2 2 , derived from von Neumann entropy subadditivity: For a quantum state ρ on ℋ = ( ℂ q ) ⊗ n , and subsystems A ( | A | < d ) and B (its complement), S ⁡ ( A ) + S ⁡ ( B ) ≥ S ⁡ ( A ​ B ) , with S ⁡ ( A ) = k ​ log ⁡ q for | A | = n − d + 1 in the purified setting, yielding the bound. For X = ℙ 1 / G , Chen-Ruan cohomology augments ordinary cohomology with twisted sectors corresponding to conjugacy classes in G ; see [ 10 ] . These sectors, associated with fixed loci X g , contribute additional cohomological dimensions, effectively enlarging the space of observables or syndromes. In the entropic framework, twisted sec
eighted projective spaces like ℙ ⁡ ( 1 , 1 , n ) , where quotient singularities tie into the entropy corrections of our conjecture. To illustrate, we derive explicit quantum code parameters from superelliptic curves. Theorem 5.3 . Let C be a superelliptic curve of level n > 2 and genus g ≥ 2 over 𝔽 q , defined by y n = f ⁡ ( x ) with deg ⁡ f = d > n + 1 . Assume C has an automorphism group containing a cyclic subgroup of order r dividing n , acting as ( x , y ) ↦ ( x , ζ ​ y ) for ζ a primitive r -th root of unity. Let P 1 , … , P m be rational places on C forming orbits under this action, with orbit sum divisor D = ∑ P i . For a divisor G invariant under the action and satisfying deg ⁡ G > 2 ​ g − 2 , the AG code C L ​ ( C , G , D ) is self-orthogonal, and the CSS construction yields a quantum code with parameters [ [ m , m − 2 ​ ( deg ⁡ G + 1 − g ) , d ] ] , where d > m − deg ⁡ G . Proof. The cyclic automorphism σ : ( x , y ) ↦ ( x , ζ ​ y ) preserves the function field 𝔽 q ​ ( C ) and acts on differentials via the residue pairing. The residue map Res : Ω C 1 → 𝔽 q at places P i satisfies Res ⁡ ( σ ∗ ​ ω ) = Res ⁡ ( ω ) for invariant differentials ω , as σ permutes places within orbits. The AG code C L ​ ( C , G , D ) evaluates functions in L ⁡ ( G ) (Riemann-Roch space) at the places in D . Since G is invariant under σ (by assumption), and D is the orbit sum, the evaluation map commutes with σ . Self-orthogonality follows from the residue theorem: For f , h ∈ L ⁡ ( G ) , the inner product ⟨ ev ⁡ ( f ) , ev ⁡ ( h ) ⟩ = ∑ Res ⁡ ( f ​ h ​ ω ) for a canonical differential ω , and invariance und
, yielding a graded ring whose Betti numbers bound the effective entropy and contribute to the correction term ϵ in our conjectured Singleton bound. Consider a chain complex C ∙ over 𝔽 q , a sequence of vector spaces with differentials ∂ i : C i → C i − 1 satisfying ∂ i − 1 ∘ ∂ i = 0 . Equipped with an inner product, the adjoint differential ∂ i † : C i − 1 → C i satisfies ⟨ ∂ i x , y ⟩ = ⟨ x , ∂ i † y ⟩ . For a quantum code, X -type stabilizers are im ( ∂ d + 1 ) ⊂ C d , Z -type stabilizers are im ( ∂ d † ) ⊂ C d , and the self-orthogonality condition im ( ∂ d + 1 ) ⊆ ker ( ∂ d † ) ensures commutativity; see [ 7 ] . For orbifold homology, let X = Y / G be a quotient variety, where Y is a smooth variety (e.g., a curve or surface) and G a finite group acting faithfully. The orbifold chain complex C ∙ orb ​ ( X ) augments the ordinary chain complex C ∙ ​ ( Y ) with twisted sectors corresponding to conjugacy classes in G , forming C ∙ orb ​ ( X ) ≅ ⨁ g ∈ G C ∙ ​ ( Y g ) C ⁡ ( g ) , where Y g is the fixed locus under g ∈ G , and C ⁡ ( g ) is the centralizer; see [ 10 ] . The homology H d orb ( X ) = ker ( ∂ d orb ) / im ( ∂ d + 1 orb ) encodes logical qubits, with twisted sectors contributing additional dimensions. The differentials on C ∙ orb ​ ( X ) are induced from the standard boundary operators on the fixed loci Y g , restricted to C ⁡ ( g ) -invariants. The nilpotency condition ∂ i − 1 ∘ ∂ i = 0 holds, as the boundary operators on each C ∙ ​ ( Y g ) satisfy ∂ 2 = 0 by construction in simplicial or cellular homology, and the direct sum and invariant projection preserve this property; see [ 10

For hypersurfaces in weighted projective spaces, the Aubry-Perret bound is particularly relevant: Theorem 5.25 (Aubry-Perret Bound) . | X ⁡ ( 𝔽 q ) | ≤ q n − 1 + d min i ⁡ w i ​ q n − 2 + ⋯ + d n − 1 ∏ i = 1 n − 1 w i + 1 . This is proven for three-dimensional cases. For weighted homogeneous polynomials, Serre’s bound gives | X ⁡ ( 𝔽 q ) | ≤ 1 + d ​ q ( n − 1 ) / 2 for smooth cases. For small q and coprime weights, these point counts yield parameters for quantum weighted AG codes, with improved distances via self-orthogonality in the CSS construction. 5.5.3. Hypersurfaces and Weil-Type Bounds Hypersurfaces in weighted projective spaces form the geometric foundation for weighted AG codes. For a quasi-smooth hypersurface X ⊂ ℙ ⁡ ( w ) of weighted degree d , the enumeration of 𝔽 q -rational poin
n of orbifold points with local isotropy groups G P i , and by ϵ = ∑ P ∈ X ( 1 − 1 | G P | ) the total orbifold defect. Conjecture 7.9 (Orbifold refinement of the quantum Singleton bound) . Let X be a quasi-smooth orbifold curve over 𝔽 q , and let D = ∑ i = 1 n P i be a sum of 𝔽 q -rational points with pairwise disjoint support. Suppose C = C L ​ ( D , G ) ⊆ 𝔽 q n is self-orthogonal, so that the associated CSS code has parameters ⟦ n , n − 2 k , d ⟧ q . Then (7.2) d ≤ k + 1 − ϵ 2 . The correction term ϵ / 2 tightens the smooth bound ( 7.1 ). When X is smooth, all isotropy groups are trivial and ϵ = 0 ; in that case ( 7.2 ) reduces to the classical inequality. For orbifold curves, the inequality predicts a strictly smaller upper bound on the attainable distance, reflecting the fact that twisted sectors remove a fractional number of degrees of freedom from the spaces of sections and differentials used to form and to analyze the code. 7.3.1. Mechanism via Orbifold Riemann–Roch The mechanism behind ( 7.2 ) can be described in the language of orbifold Riemann–Roch. Write ℓ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) = dim 𝔽 q ℒ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) for the dimension of the Riemann–Roch space on X . In the smooth case, for divisors of sufficiently high degree ( deg > 2 ​ g − 2 ), one has ℓ ⁡ ( G ) = deg ⁡ ( G ) − g + 1 , and, dually, ℓ ⁡ ( K X − G ) = deg ⁡ ( K X − G ) − g + 1 . For orbifold curves, these equalities acquire a deficit that is the sum of local contributions from the isotropy. The orbifold Riemann–Roch theorem takes the form: (7.3) ℓ ⁡ ( G ) = deg ⁡ ( G ) − g + 1 − δ ⁡ ( G ) , ℓ ⁡ ( K X − G ) = deg ⁡ ( K X − G ) − g + 1 − δ ⁡ ( K X −
( K X − G ) 2 . Since the maximum total defect for any divisor is bounded by the sum of local defects ϵ , we obtain the conjectured bound: d ≤ k + 1 − ϵ 2 . The heart of the matter is that in the orbifold setting, both the primal and the dual linear systems lose dimension by amounts controlled by the same local isotropy data; in the CSS balance these two losses average to the correction ϵ / 2 . Example 7.10 . Consider a hyperelliptic curve X : y 2 = f 6 ​ ( x , z ) ⊂ ℙ ⁡ ( 1 , 1 , 2 ) . The ambient singular locus of ℙ ⁡ ( 1 , 1 , 2 ) contains the single point Q = [ 0 : 0 : 1 ] with isotropy group μ 2 . For a general binary form f 6 , the curve X does not pass through Q (since f 6 ​ ( 0 , 0 ) = 0 implies singular coefficients or reducibility). Thus, X is a smooth curve located in the smooth locus of the weighted plane. Consequently, ϵ = 0 , and the refined bound reduces to the classical bound d ≤ k + 1 . Example 7.11 . In contrast, consider the quasi-smooth curve X : z 2 + x 2 ​ y 2 + x 5 = 0 ⊂ ℙ ⁡ ( 2 , 3 , 5 ) . We analyze the intersection of X with the singular loci of the ambient space ℙ ⁡ ( 2 , 3 , 5 ) : • The point P y = [ 0 : 1 : 0 ] has weights proportional to 1 / 3 (isotropy μ 3 ). Substituting into X : 0 + 0 + 0 = 0 . Thus P y ∈ X . • The point P z = [ 0 : 0 : 1 ] has weights proportional to 1 / 5 (isotropy μ 5 ). Substituting into X : 1 + 0 + 0 ≠ 0 . Thus P z ∉ X . • The point P x = [ 1 : 0 : 0 ] has weights proportional to 1 / 2 (isotropy μ 2 ). Substituting into X : 0 + 0 + 1 ≠ 0 . Thus P x ∉ X . The curve meets the ambient singular locus only at [ 0 : 1 : 0 ] , where the isotropy 
efining polynomial, illustrating how orbifold points constrain the code parameters by fractional units. A full proof of Conjecture 7.9 for arbitrary quasi-smooth orbifold curves would require cohomological control of the deficits δ ⁡ ( G ) and δ ⁡ ( K X − G ) and their compatibility with the residue pairing that underlies AG duality. In families where the orbifold Riemann–Roch formula can be made explicit (for instance, weighted projective lines and certain Delsarte curves), the bound is consistent with computations and specializes to the classical quantum Singleton inequality when ϵ = 0 . 7.4. Homological Construction and Partial Proof of the Refined Singleton Bound Weighted gradings on a quasi-smooth curve X ⊂ ℙ ⁡ ( w ) naturally induce bigraded chain complexes whose boundary maps furnish the requisite stabilizer checks. While the standard algebraic geometry code construction is often described via evaluation maps, it can be rigorously formulated as a homological complex. This perspective not only clarifies the duality properties required for the CSS construction but also provides the mechanism for deriving the refined quantum Singleton bound. The argument below establishes the refined Singleton inequality under the geometric hypotheses introduced in this chapter; the full generality of Conjecture 7.9 remains open. 7.4.1. The Evaluation Chain Complex Let X be a quasi-smooth projective curve over 𝔽 q embedded in a weighted projective space ℙ ⁡ ( w ) . For any divisor D , let ℒ ⁡ ( D ) = H 0 ​ ( X , 𝒪 X ​ ( D ) ) denote the corresponding Riemann–Roch space. Let 𝒫 = { P 1 , … , P n } ⊂ X ⁡ ( 𝔽 q ) be a set of distinct rational points, which we interpret as the physical qubits of the code. We identify the space of physical states with 𝔽 q n = ⨁ i = 1 n 𝔽 q ⋅ e P i . To construct a CSS code, we require classical codes C X and C Z satisfying C X ⟂ ⊆ C Z . These arise naturally from a short complex of vector spaces determined by the geometry of X . Let G be a divisor on X . We define the evaluation complex 0 ⟶ ℒ ⁡ ( K X − G ) → ∂ Z 𝔽 q n → ∂ X ℒ ​ ( G ) ∗ ⟶ 0 . Since X is Gorenstein, we identify ℒ ⁡ ( K X − G ) with Ω ⁡ ( − G ) , so its elements may be viewed as rational differentials. Thus ∂ X ( v ) is the linear functional on ℒ ⁡ ( G ) determined by pairing with evaluation vectors. These maps satisfy ∂ X ∘ ∂ Z = 0 . ( 1 , 0 ) + b ⁡ ( 1 2 , 3 2 ) , where a , b ∈ ℤ . We discuss the problem in triangular coordinates due to the following conjecture by Erdős and Fishburn 6 . Conjecture 1 . For any m ≥ 3 , at least one of the maximum m -distance sets lies within triangular lattices. If m ≥ 7 , then maximum m -distance sets can only be found in triangular lattices. For the cases of m ≤ 6 , the above conjecture is true. There are some recent studies on the cases of triangular lattices. Most of them are based on special hexagonal figures. Balaji, Edwards, Loftin, Mcharo, Phillips, Rice, and Tsegaye gave a bound with regular polygons and especially regular hexagons in triangular lattices 3 . Ahmed and Wildstrom used some hexagons that are symmetrical with respect to vertical lines and bounded the number of distances inside them 2 . Here, we determine the maximum m - distance sets from m = 7 to 34 under the assumption of using the m smallest distances in the triangular lattices. We also present an alternate method of construction by equiangular hexagons. If we use equiangular hexagons instead of regular hexagons, we can find constructions of larger size than the case found in 3 . The following values are the best lower bounds we found for sets that determine at most m distances by above two methods. these are in fact maximum when we are discussing distance sets based on the m smallest distances, we have the following conjecture Conjecture 2 . The maximum 7 -distance and 8 -distance sets have 16 and 19 points, respectively. The 7 -distance set can only be realized by taking all triangular lattices in a regular hexagon with side length 2 , and removing three all adjacent or all non-adjacent corners. The 8 -distance set can only be realized by taking all points in that regular hexagon. However, when we construct the 19 -distance set, we noticed that the smallest 19 distances cannot form a maximum size 19 -distance set. The 19 smallest distances would only yield a 19 -distance set of 45 points, while an equiangular hexagon with side length alternating 3 and 4 will form a 19 -distance set of 48 points. The main reason is that when we construct the 19 -distance set by the 19 smallest distances, the 19 th smallest distance only has multiplicity 2 . The hexagon eliminated this distance and added the 20 th smallest one which is 7 , resulting in a construction with 3 more points. This observation gives two other conjectures. Conjecture 3 . Taking all points in an equiangular hexagon with side length alternating k and k + 1 , or a regular hexagon, will always give a maximum m-distance set. There are two confirmed examples for this conjecture: All triangular lattice points in a regular hexagon with side length 1 give a maximum 3 -distance set, and all triangular lattice points in an equiangular hexagon alternating side lengths 1 and 2 give the unique maximum 5 -distance set. Conjecture 4 . For any m ≥ 3 , at least one construction of maximum m -distance set can be realized by repeatedly removing points from the aforementioned hexagons. The method is based on the paper of Szöllősi and Östergård 11 . Algorithm 2.0.1 . Given a set 𝒮 with m distances, we will consider the two points A ⁡ ( 0 , 0 ) , B ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) , and find all points P such that d ⁡ ( P , A ) ∈ 𝒮 and d ⁡ ( P , B ) ∈ 𝒮 . We would put these points in a set called V . Now we build a graph with the vertex set V , and two vertices share an edge if the distance between the two points is in 𝒮 . Finally, by finding the largest clique W , we have W ∪ { A , B } being an m -distance set. Proof.. We know that any two points in the largest clique have a distance in 𝒮 , and any point in the largest clique also has a distance to A and B in 𝒮 . Since | 𝒮 | = m we know that any two points among the construction have at most m possible distances. ∎ By setting the distances in 𝒮 to the distances in the triangular lattice, we can find the largest m -distance set by building the graph and running a maximum clique algorithm on the graph. If the construction is based on the hexagon construction, then every entry of the multiplicity array would be a multiple of 3 . This can be easily seen by the symmetry of the hexagon and the removed points. 4.3 Constructions with Hexagons First, we will see why we run the previous algorithm with the smallest 19 distances, and can not construct the hexagons for m = 19 . Property 4.3.1 . ( k , k + 1 ) -equiangular hexagons and regular hexagons cannot be constructed with the smallest m -distances when m ≥ 19 . Proof. When m = 19 , the longest diagonal has length 7 , so we would consider the case that the length L of the diagonal is at least 7 . The largest distance in this hexagon is L , and the second largest is ( L − 1 2 ) 2 + 3 4 = L 2 − L + 1 . Note that the length ( L − 1 2 ) 2 + ( 3 ​ 3 2 ) 2 = L 2 − L + 8 is less than L when L ≥ 8 . Therefore, for m ≥ 23 , the hexagon would miss a distance between its two largest distances. m = 19 can be checked by the picture in the previous subsections. ∎ Therefore, we might consider to construct m -distance sets by the shape of hexagons. Property 4.3.2 (Hexagon Constructions) . If we want to build an m -distance set, we find the smallest m ′ ≥ m such that there exists a ( k , k + 1 ) -equiangular hexagon or regular hexagon forming an m ′ -distance set, and r
e 24 -distance 26 -distance 27 -distance 28 -distance 30 -distance 32 -distance 33 -distance 34 -distance Therefore, we conjecture that the construction in the above table of hexagon would be the maximum. 5 Conclusion and Further Discussions 5.1 Conclusion on the Two Methods Conclusion 5.1 . The construction by the method of maximum clique with the first m smallest distances gives the largest construction, except for m = 19 , 23 , 28 , 29 , 34 . Therefore, the clique method works for most of the cases. Conclusion 5.2 . The construction by using the first m smallest distances in triangular lattices can not attain the maximum size for m = 19 , 23 , 28 , 29 , 34 , since the construction of hexagons can have more points. These ones often have the maximum clique algorithm finding the same answer with the m − 1 smallest distances, because the multiplicity of the m th distances are all 3 or less. When the m -distance set construction of hexagon is larger than the construction by maximum clique, the construction of maximum clique for m -distance set often has the same size as ( m − 1 ) -distance set since the largest distance has small multiplicity. When we compare our constructions to the ones in 3 , we noticed that some of our constructions are larger. For the 18 -distance set, the paper removed points from a regular hexagon with side length 4 to get a 43 points construction. On the contrary, we removed three corners from a ( 3 , 4 ) -equiangular hexagon to get a 45 points construction. There was also a 70 points construction for 29 -distance set in that paper. However, we were able to construct a s
with line symmetry. 5.2 Further Discussions Comparing the two methods, the construction of hexagons works better, so we conjecture that the construction of hexagons will attain the maximum i.e. all triangular lattices in a regular hexagon, or a ( k , k + 1 ) -equiangular hexagon will form a maximum m -distance set. In addition, there is a stronger version that can be used on every m : if m ≥ 3 then at least one construction of maximum m -distance set can be found by repeatedly removing points from a regular hexagon or a ( k , k + 1 ) -equiangular hexagon. Also, we made a conjecture based on some non-optimal construction presented by the maximum clique algorithm. For all m ≥ 3 , any maximum m -distance set does not have a distance with multiplicity less than 3 . Finally, for the 7 -distance and 8 -distance construction, we also conjecture that the maximum 7 -distance set in triangular lattices has 16 points, and the maximum 8 -distance set in triangular lattices has 19 points. In addition, they are only realized by the constructions found in subsection 2.1 and 2.4. If Erdős’s conjecture is true, then these would be the only maximum 7 and 8 distance sets in ℝ 2 . oliner, Krivelevich, and Michaeli [ 19 ] introduced an oriented analogue of graph discrepancy for Hamilton cycles. They conjectured a generalisation of Dirac’s theorem, which was later proved by Freschi and Lo [ 15 ] . Theorem 1.2 ( [ 15 ] ) . Let G be an oriented graph on n ≥ 3 vertices. If δ ⁡ ( G ) ≥ n 2 then there exists a Hamilton cycle C in G such that σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ δ ⁡ ( G ) . Freschi and Lo further proposed an Ore-type extension of Theorem 1.2 as an open problem in [ 15 ] . The Ore-type condition is captured by the parameter σ 2 ( G ) = min { d ( x ) + d ( y ) ∣ x , y ∈ V ( G ) and x , y are non-adjacent } , where x , y are non-adjacent means that there is no edge between them. The problem can be stated as follows. Problem 1.3 ( [ 15 ] ) . Let G be an oriented graph on n vertices. Suppose that σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n . What lower bound on σ max ​ ( C ) can be guaranteed for a Hamilton cycle C in G ? Our main result provides an answer to this problem for sufficiently large n . It can be viewed as an extension of Ore’s theorem [ 25 ] to the setting of oriented discrepancy. Theorem 1.4 . For every γ > 0 , there exists a positive integer n 0 such that the following holds. Let G be an oriented graph on n ≥ n 0 vertices with σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n . Then G contains a Hamilton cycle C such that σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ max ⁡ { n 2 , σ 2 ​ ( G ) 2 − γ ​ n } . The bound of σ max ​ ( C ) in Theorem 1.4 is best possible: under the same condition σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n , one cannot guarantee a Hamilton cycle C with σ max ​ ( C ) > σ 2 ​ ( G ) / 2 . Indeed, Theorem 1.4 reduces to a generalization of Theorem 1.2 for sufficientl
 of tournaments. Recently, Guo et al. [ 20 ] considered a similar problem from a different perspective. They proposed a conjecture (Conjecture 1.3 in [ 20 ] ) that under the condition σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n , every oriented graph on n vertices contains a Hamilton cycle C with σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ max ⁡ { δ ⁡ ( G ) , n − δ ⁡ ( G ) } . As a partial result, they proved that if 1 < δ ⁡ ( G ) < n / 2 and σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n + δ ⁡ ( G ) − 2 , then a Hamilton cycle exists with σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ n − δ ⁡ ( G ) . Organization: The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Subsections 2.1 and 2.2 , we introduce notation and present an extremal construction showing that the bound of σ max ​ ( C ) in Theorem 1.4 is best possible. Subsection 2.3 provides an overview of the proof of Theorem 1.4 . In Section 3 we present some auxiliary lemmas, also introduce some useful tools including Diregularity Lemma. Next, Subsection 4.1 presents key absorbing, connecting, and almost covering lemmas needed for the proof of Theorem 1.4 , and Subsection 4.2 completes the proof. Finally, we make some concluding remarks in Section 5 . 2 Preliminaries and proof overview 2.1 Notation Let G be a digraph, and let U ⁡ ( G ) denote its underlying graph, obtained from G by ignoring the directions of its edges. An oriented graph is a digraph without loops such that between every two vertices there is at most one edge. The number of vertices | G | := | V ⁡ ( G ) | is called the order of G , and the number of edges e ⁡ ( G ) := | E ⁡ ( G ) | is called the size of G . Given two vertices x and y of G , we write x ​ y for the edge directed from x to y

Moreover, to make Theorem 1.4 more complete, we define σ 2 ​ ( G ) = 2 ​ ( n − 1 ) for tournaments in this paper, a definition that may deviate from those adopted in other relevant literature. It is a fundamental result in tournament that every tournament contains a Hamilton path; based on this, our theorem remains valid in the context of tournaments. Recently, Ai et al. Our main result provides an asymptotically affirmative answer to this conjecture for all sufficiently large n . It can be viewed as an extension of Ore’s theorem [ 25 ] to the setting of oriented discrepancy. Theorem 1.4 . For every γ > 0 , there exists a positive integer n 0 such that the following holds. Let G be an oriented graph on n ≥ n 0 vertices with σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n . Then G contains a Hamilton cycle C such that σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ max ⁡ { n 2 , σ 2 ​ ( G ) 2 − γ ​ n } . The bound of σ max ​ ( C ) in Theorem 1.4 is best possible: under the same condition σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n , one cannot guarantee a Hamilton cycle C with σ max ​ ( C ) > σ 2 ​ ( G ) / 2 . Indeed, Theorem 1.4 reduces to a generalization of Theorem 1.2 for sufficiently large n when the error term γ ​ n is omitted. Moreover, to make Theorem 1.4 more complete, we define σ 2 ​ ( G ) = 2 ​ ( n − 1 ) for tournaments in this paper, a definition that may deviate from those adopted in other relevant literature. It is a fundamental result in tournament that every tournament contains a Hamilton path; based on this, our theorem remains valid in the context of tournaments. Recently, Ai et al. [ 1 ] also considered a similar problem from a different perspective. They proposed a conjecture (Conjecture 1.2 in [ 1 ] ) that under the condition σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n ,

It is worth noting that although the absorption method is used here, a structural approach might also be applicable to this problem. However, a direct application of the structural method from [ 16 ] does not suffice to answer Conjecture 1.3 . Several aspects remain open and warrant further investigation. Regarding Theorem 1.4 , determining the precise value of σ max ​ ( C ) presents a challenging open problem. Furthermore, since a cycle factor naturally generalizes the notion of a Hamilton cycle, it is plausible that an analogous statement holds in this broader setting. This leads to the following conjecture, which can be viewed as an extension of the El-Zahar conjecture [ 11 ] . Conjecture 5.1 . Keywords: Oriented graphs, Hamilton cycles, Oriented discrepancy, Ore-type conditions Mathematics Subject Classifications: 05C20; 05C45; 05C38 1 Introduction Graph discrepancy, introduced by Erdős and Spencer [ 10 , 12 ] , extends classical discrepancy theory by studying the unavoidable imbalance in two-colorings of edges. In a foundational result, Erdős, Füredi, Loebl, and Sós [ 11 ] proved that there exists an absolute constant c > 0 such that, for every n -vertex tree T with maximum degree Δ , every 2-edge coloring of E ⁡ ( K n ) contains a copy of T having at least ( n − 1 ) / 2 + c ⁡ ( n − 1 − Δ ) edges of one color. A central and actively studied question asks: for a two-edge-colored host graph G , what sufficient conditions ensure the existence of a given subgraph H with large discrepancy? Balogh, Csaba, Jing and Pluhár [ 3 ] initiated the study of discrepancy for Hamilton cycles in dense graphs, and this work can be regarded as a discrepancy analogue of Dirac’s theorem [ 8 ] . Theorem 1.1 ( [ 3 ] ) . Let 0 < c < 1 / 4 and n ∈ ℕ be sufficiently large. If G is a 2 -edge-colored graph on n vertices with δ ⁡ ( G ) ≥ ( 3 / 4 + c ) ​ n , then G contains a Hamilton cycle with at least ( 1 / 2 + c / 64 ) ​ n edges in one color. In recent years, this problem has attracted considerable attention and has been investigated for a wide range of subgraph types, such as perfect matchings and Hamilton cycles [ 3 , 13 ,
logous notions are defined for oriented paths. Motivated by Theorem 1.1 , Gishboliner, Krivelevich, and Michaeli [ 18 ] conjectured a generalization of Dirac’s theorem, which was later proved by Freschi and Lo [ 14 ] . Theorem 1.2 ( [ 14 ] ) . Let G be an oriented graph on n ≥ 3 vertices. If δ ⁡ ( G ) ≥ n 2 then there exists a Hamilton cycle C in G such that σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ δ ⁡ ( G ) . Freschi and Lo [ 14 ] asked whether their theorem admits an Ore-type analogue. For a graph or oriented graph G , define σ 2 ( G ) := min x , y ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) { d ( x ) + d ( y ) ∣ x ≠ y , x y , y x ∉ E ( G ) } , and for a complete graph or tournament set σ 2 ​ ( G ) := 2 ​ ( n − 1 ) . Ai et al. [ 1 ] subsequently formulated the following precise conjecture. Conjecture 1.3 ( [ 1 ] ) . Let G be an oriented graph on n ≥ 3 vertices. If σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n , then G contains a Hamilton cycle C such that σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ σ 2 ​ ( G ) / 2 . Ai et al. [ 1 ] also gave constructions showing that, under the hypothesis σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n , one cannot guarantee σ max ​ ( C ) > σ 2 ​ ( G ) / 2 . Thus the conjectured bound is best possible. Our main result provides an asymptotic affirmative answer to this conjecture for all sufficiently large n . Thus, Theorem 1.4 can be viewed as an asymptotic oriented-discrepancy analogue of Ore’s theorem [ 22 ] . Theorem 1.4 . For every γ > 0 , there exists a positive integer n 0 such that the following holds. Let G be an oriented graph on n ≥ n 0 vertices with σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n . Then G contains a Hamilton cycle C such that σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ max ⁡ { n 2 , σ 2 ​ ( G ) 2 − γ ​ n } . Up to an additive o ⁡ ( n

e proof. 5 Concluding remarks In this paper, we use the absorption method to obtain an asymptotic affirmative answer to Conjecture 1.3 of Ai et al. It would be interesting to determine whether the structural approach developed in [ 14 ] can be adapted to the Ore-type setting and, in particular, whether it can lead to an exact version of Theorem 1.4 . Since cycle factors generalize Hamilton cycles, it is natural to ask whether a corresponding oriented-discrepancy statement holds for prescribed cycle factors. This leads to the following oriented-discrepancy analogue of the El-Zahar conjecture [ 9 ] . Conjecture 5.1 . Let G be an oriented graph on n = n 1 + n 2 + ⋯ + n k vertices, where n i ≥ 3 for each 1 ≤ i ≤ k . If δ ⁡ ( G ) ≥ ∑ i = 1 k ⌈ n i / 2 ⌉ , then G contains a cycle factor 𝒞 = { C 1 , C 2 , … , C k } satisfying σ max ​ ( 𝒞 ) ≥ δ ⁡ ( G ) − ( k − 1 ) with | C i | = n i . The bound in Conjecture 5.1 , if true, would be best possible. Indeed, let G be a transitive tournament on n vertices. Then δ ⁡ ( G ) = n − 1 . Since a transitive tournament contains no directed cycle, every oriented cycle C i in G satisfies σ max ​ ( C i ) ≤ | C i | − 1 . Consequently, every cycle factor 𝒞 = { C 1 , … , C k } with | C i | = n i satisfies σ max ​ ( 𝒞 ) ≤ ∑ i = 1 k ( n i − 1 ) = n − k = δ ⁡ ( G ) − ( k − 1 ) . Acknowledgment.  prescribed cycle factors. This leads to the following oriented-discrepancy analogue of the El-Zahar conjecture [ 9 ] . Conjecture 5.1 . Let G be an oriented graph on n = n 1 + n 2 + ⋯ + n k vertices, where n i ≥ 3 for each 1 ≤ i ≤ k . If δ ⁡ ( G ) ≥ ∑ i = 1 k ⌈ n i / 2 ⌉ , then G contains a cycle factor 𝒞 = { C 1 , C 2 , … , C k } satisfying σ max ​ ( 𝒞 ) ≥ δ ⁡ ( G ) − ( k − 1 ) with | C i | = n i . The bound in Conjecture 5.1 , if true, would be best possible. Indeed, let G be a transitive tournament on n vertices. Then δ ⁡ ( G ) = n − 1 . Since a transitive tournament contains no directed cycle, every oriented cycle C i in G satisfies σ max ​ ( C i ) ≤ | C i | − 1 . Consequently, every cycle factor 𝒞 = { C 1 , … , C k } with | C i | = n i satisfies σ max ​ ( 𝒞 ) ≤ ∑ i = 1 k ( n i − 1 ) = n − k = δ ⁡ ( G ) − ( k − 1 ) . Acknowledgment. Keywords: Oriented graphs, Hamilton cycles, oriented discrepancy, Ore-type conditions Mathematics Subject Classifications: 05C20; 05C45; 05C38 1 Introduction Graph discrepancy, introduced by Erdős and Spencer [ 10 , 12 ] , extends classical discrepancy theory by studying the unavoidable imbalance in two-colorings of edges. Erdős, Füredi, Loebl, and Sós [ 11 ] showed that there is a constant c > 0 such that any 2-edge coloring of K n admits, for every n -vertex tree T of maximum degree Δ , a copy of T with at least ( n − 1 ) / 2 + c ⁡ ( n − 1 − Δ ) edges of the same color. This motivates the broader problem of determining conditions on a 2-edge-colored graph G that guarantee a copy of a given subgraph H with large discrepancy. The investigation of Hamilton cycle discrepancy in dense graphs can be traced back to the work of Balogh, Csaba, Jing, and Pluhár [ 3 ] . Theorem 1.1 ( [ 3 ] ) . Let 0 < c < 1 / 4 and n ∈ ℕ be sufficiently large. If G is a 2 -edge-colored graph on n vertices with δ ⁡ ( G ) ≥ ( 3 / 4 + c ) ​ n , then G contains a Hamilton cycle with at least ( 1 / 2 + c / 64 ) ​ n edges in one color. The study of this problem has developed in several directions over the past few years. Results concerning perfect matchings and Hamilton cycles can be found in [ 3 , 13 , 15 , 16 , 17 , 18 ] , while [ 17 ] , [ 4 , 6 ] and [ 5 ] deal with spanning trees, H -factors and powers of Hamilton cycles, respectively. G
logous notions are defined for oriented paths. Motivated by Theorem 1.1 , Gishboliner, Krivelevich, and Michaeli [ 18 ] conjectured a generalization of Dirac’s theorem. This conjecture was subsequently confirmed by Freschi and Lo [ 14 ] . Theorem 1.2 ( [ 14 ] ) . Let G be an oriented graph on n ≥ 3 vertices. If δ ⁡ ( G ) ≥ n 2 then there exists a Hamilton cycle C in G such that σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ δ ⁡ ( G ) . Freschi and Lo [ 14 ] asked whether their theorem admits an Ore-type analogue. For a graph or oriented graph G , define σ 2 ( G ) := min x , y ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) { d ( x ) + d ( y ) ∣ x ≠ y , x y , y x ∉ E ( G ) } , and for a complete graph or tournament set σ 2 ​ ( G ) := 2 ​ ( n − 1 ) . Ai et al. [ 1 ] subsequently formulated the following precise conjecture. Conjecture 1.3 ( [ 1 ] ) . Let G be an oriented graph on n ≥ 3 vertices. If σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n , then G contains a Hamilton cycle C such that σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ σ 2 ​ ( G ) / 2 . Ai et al. [ 1 ] also gave constructions showing that, under the hypothesis σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n , one cannot guarantee σ max ​ ( C ) > σ 2 ​ ( G ) / 2 . Thus the conjectured bound is best possible. Our main theorem confirms an asymptotic version of this conjecture when n is sufficiently large. Thus, Theorem 1.4 can be viewed as an asymptotic oriented-discrepancy analogue of Ore’s theorem [ 22 ] . Theorem 1.4 . For every γ > 0 , there exists a positive integer n 0 such that the following holds. Let G be an oriented graph on n ≥ n 0 vertices with σ 2 ​ ( G ) ≥ n . Then G contains a Hamilton cycle C such that σ max ​ ( C ) ≥ max ⁡ { n 2 , σ 2 ​ ( G ) 2 − γ ​ n } . Up to an additiv

e proof. 5 Concluding remarks In this paper, we use the absorption method to obtain an asymptotic affirmative answer to Conjecture 1.3 of Ai et al. It would be interesting to determine whether the structural approach developed in [ 14 ] can be adapted to the Ore-type setting and, in particular, whether it can lead to an exact version of Theorem 1.4 . Since cycle factors generalize Hamilton cycles, it is natural to ask whether a corresponding oriented-discrepancy statement holds for prescribed cycle factors. This leads to the following oriented-discrepancy analogue of the El-Zahar conjecture [ 9 ] . Conjecture 5.1 . Let G be an oriented graph on n = n 1 + n 2 + ⋯ + n k vertices, where n i ≥ 3 for each 1 ≤ i ≤ k . If δ ⁡ ( G ) ≥ ∑ i = 1 k ⌈ n i / 2 ⌉ , then G contains a cycle factor 𝒞 = { C 1 , C 2 , … , C k } satisfying σ max ​ ( 𝒞 ) ≥ δ ⁡ ( G ) − ( k − 1 ) with | C i | = n i . The bound in Conjecture 5.1 , if true, would be best possible. Indeed, let G be a transitive tournament on n vertices. Then δ ⁡ ( G ) = n − 1 . Since a transitive tournament contains no directed cycle, every oriented cycle C i in G satisfies σ max ​ ( C i ) ≤ | C i | − 1 . Consequently, every cycle factor 𝒞 = { C 1 , … , C k } with | C i | = n i satisfies σ max ​ ( 𝒞 ) ≤ ∑ i = 1 k ( n i − 1 ) = n − k = δ ⁡ ( G ) − ( k − 1 ) . Acknowledgment. We are grateful to Václav Rozhoň and Robert Samal for correcting the right statement of Conjecture 5.1 . References [1] J. Ai, Q. Guo, G. Gutin, Y. Lan, Q. Shao, A. Yeo, Y. rences License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2605.14176v1 [math.CO] 13 May 2026 Counterexamples to a Conjecture on Laplacian Ratios of Trees Priyanshu Pant † † thanks: Indian Institute of Technology Indore; website: https://sites.google.com/view/priyanshupant . Email: priyanshupant03@gmail.com Abstract For a graph G with no isolated vertices, its Laplacian ratio is defined as π ⁡ ( G ) = per ⁡ ( L ⁡ ( G ) ) ∏ v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) d ⁡ ( v ) , where L ⁡ ( G ) is the Laplacian matrix of G , d ⁡ ( v ) is the degree of v , and per denotes the permanent. Brualdi and Goldwasser [ 1 ] asked for the maximum value of π ⁡ ( T ) among trees T with a fixed number of vertices. Wu, Dong and Lai [ 2 ] recently proposed a conjectural answer to this problem. We give infinite families of counterexamples to their conjecture. Keywords. Laplacian matrix, permanent, tree, matching, extremal graph. 1 Introduction All graphs in this paper are finite and simple. For a graph G , we write V ⁡ ( G ) and E ⁡ ( G ) for its vertex set and edge set. For a vertex v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) , let d ⁡ ( v ) denote its degree. We write A ⁡ ( G ) for the adjacency matrix of G , and D ⁡ ( G ) for the diagonal matrix whose v -th diagonal entry is d ⁡ ( v ) . The Laplacian matrix of G is L ⁡ ( G ) = D ⁡ ( G ) − A ⁡ ( G ) . For an n × n matrix M = ( m i ​ j ) , its permanent is per ⁡ ( M ) = ∑ σ ∈ S n ∏ i = 1 n m i , σ ⁡ ( i ) , where S n is the symmetric group on { 1 , … , n } . If G has no isolated vertices, its Laplacian ratio is defined by π ⁡ ( G ) = per ⁡ ( L ⁡ ( G ) ) ∏ v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) d ⁡ ( v ) . Brualdi and Goldwasser [ 1 ] init
Lai [ 2 ] recently proposed a conjectural answer to this problem. We give infinite families of counterexamples to their conjecture. Keywords. Laplacian matrix, permanent, tree, matching, extremal graph. 1 Introduction All graphs in this paper are finite and simple. For a graph G , we write V ⁡ ( G ) and E ⁡ ( G ) for its vertex set and edge set. For a vertex v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) , let d ⁡ ( v ) denote its degree. We write A ⁡ ( G ) for the adjacency matrix of G , and D ⁡ ( G ) for the diagonal matrix whose v -th diagonal entry is d ⁡ ( v ) . The Laplacian matrix of G is L ⁡ ( G ) = D ⁡ ( G ) − A ⁡ ( G ) . For an n × n matrix M = ( m i ​ j ) , its permanent is per ⁡ ( M ) = ∑ σ ∈ S n ∏ i = 1 n m i , σ ⁡ ( i ) , where S n is the symmetric group on { 1 , … , n } . If G has no isolated vertices, its Laplacian ratio is defined by π ⁡ ( G ) = per ⁡ ( L ⁡ ( G ) ) ∏ v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) d ⁡ ( v ) . Brualdi and Goldwasser [ 1 ] initiated the study of permanents of Laplacian matrices of trees and bipartite graphs. For trees, they proved the sharp bounds 2 ​ ( n − 1 ) ≤ per ⁡ ( L ⁡ ( T ) ) ≤ per ⁡ ( L ⁡ ( P n ) ) , where T is a tree on n vertices and P n is the path on n vertices. The left equality holds if and only if T is a star, and the right equality holds if and only if T is a path. The upper bound can be written explicitly as per ⁡ ( L ⁡ ( P n ) ) = ( 1 − 2 2 ) ​ ( 1 + 2 ) n + ( 1 + 2 2 ) ​ ( 1 − 2 ) n . Brualdi and Goldwasser also proved that every tree T satisfies π ⁡ ( T ) ≥ 2 , with equality if and only if T is a star. They then asked for extremal results for the Laplacian ratio of trees. ith a fixed number of vertices. Wu, Dong and Lai [ 2 ] recently studied this maximum problem and proposed the following conjecture. Conjecture 1.1 (Wu–Dong–Lai, Conjecture 1.2 [ 2 ] ) . Let T be a tree on n vertices. (i) If n is odd, then π ⁡ ( T ) ≤ 2 ​ ( 3 2 ) ( n − 3 ) / 2 , with equality if and only if T = S ⁡ ( n , n − 1 2 ) . (ii) If n = 4 ​ k , then π ⁡ ( T ) ≤ 4 ​ n 2 + 8 ​ n + 24 n ⁡ ( n + 4 ) ​ ( 3 2 ) ( n − 6 ) / 2 , with equality if and only if T = S ⁡ ( n , n + 2 2 , n − 2 2 ) . (iii) If n = 4 ​ k + 2 , then π ⁡ ( T ) ≤ 4 ​ n 2 + 8 ​ n + 40 ( n + 2 ) 2 ​ ( 3 2 ) ( n − 6 ) / 2 , with equality if and only if T = S ⁡ ( n , n 2 , n 2 ) . Here S ⁡ ( n , ( n − 1 ) / 2 ) is the subdivided star obtained from the star on ( n + 1 ) / 2 vertices by attaching one pendant edge to each noncentral vertex. The tree S ⁡ ( n , a , b ) is obtained from two such subdivided stars with a and b vertices by adding an edge between their centers. ⋯ S ⁡ ( n , ( n − 1 ) / 2 ) ⋯ ⋯ S ⁡ ( n , a , b ) Figure 1: The trees S ⁡ ( n , ( n − 1 ) / 2 ) and S ⁡ ( n , a , b ) appearing in the Wu–Dong–Lai conjecture. We show that this conjecture is false in all three cases. The counterexamples come from a family of trees T ⁡ ( a 1 , … , a m ) , defined in Section 2 . For this family we obtain an explicit formula π ⁡ ( T ⁡ ( a 1 , … , a m ) ) = ( 3 2 ) a 1 + ⋯ + a m ​ f m , where f m is given by a simple two-term recurrence. Applying this formula to the subfamilies T ⁡ ( 3 , t , 3 ) , T ⁡ ( t , t , t , t ) , T ⁡ ( t , t , t + 1 , t ) ( t ≥ 4 ) , gives strict counterexamples to the three proposed upper bounds. Determining the exact maximum value of the Laplacian ratio among n -vertex trees remains open. References [1] R. A. Brualdi and J. L. Dong, and H.-J. Lai (2025) Two problems on Laplacian ratios of trees . Discrete Applied Mathematics 372 , pp. 224–236 . External Links: Document Cited by: Conjecture 1.1 , §1 , §4 , Abstract . finition, name=Example, sibling=theorem] [style=definition, name=Open Problem, sibling=theorem] [style=definition, name=Conjecture, sibling=theorem] Extremal t -intersecting Families of Permutations for Large t Pitchayut Saengrungkongka Address: Department of Mathematics, Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Cambridge, MA 02139, USA Email address: pitchayutbcc168@gmail.com Abstract. A set of permutations of { 1 , 2 , … , n } is t -intersecting if any two permutations agree on at least t inputs. A recent work by Kupavskii, in the spirit of the Erdős–Ko–Rado Theorem, shows that for all t ≤ n − O ⁡ ( n ​ log ⁡ log ​ n log ⁡ n ) , every t -intersecting family of permutations of { 1 , 2 , … , n } with the maximum size must be isomorphic to the set 𝒜 k = { σ : σ ⁡ ( i ) = i ​ for at least ​ t + k ​ indices ​ i ∈ { 1 , 2 , … , t + 2 ​ k } } for some k . By refining Kupavskii’s spread approximation technique, we prove that this conclusion holds for a wider range of t ≤ n − n 5 / 7 + ε . 1. Introduction One of the classical results in extremal set theory is the Erdős–Ko–Rado Theorem [ EKR61 ] . It states that for all positive integers n and r such that n ≥ 2 ​ r and for any family ℱ ⊆ ( [ n ] r ) such that any two sets in ℱ have a nonempty intersection, we have | ℱ | ≤ ( n − 1 r − 1 ) . In particular, the equality is achieved when ℱ consists of all sets containing 1 . Since then, this result has been generalized in many different directions, leading to numerous variants of problems about intersecting families. One way to generalize the Erdős–Ko–Rado Theorem is to consider t -intersecting families. A 
fix 1 , 2 , … , t (so it has size ( n − t ) ! ). Analogous to Ahlswede and Khachatrian’s theorem, we have the following conjecture. 1 . If ℱ ⊆ Σ n is a t -intersecting family, then | ℱ | ≤ max k ⁡ | 𝒜 k | . Moreover, if | ℱ | = max k ⁡ | 𝒜 k | , then ℱ = σ ​ 𝒜 k ​ τ for some k and permutations σ and τ . In particular, by comparing | 𝒜 k | across different k , the conjecture implies that 𝒜 0 is the maximum family for all t ≤ n 2 . The most recent progress towards resolving this conjecture is due to Kupavskii, who showed [ Kup26+ , Thm. 1] that the conjecture holds for t ≤ ( 1 − o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ n , together with a sharp stability result. Upon closer inspection (cf. Remark 1 ), his result holds for all t ≤ n − O ⁡ ( n ​ log ⁡ log ​ n log ⁡ n ) . By enhancing this result with several new ideas, namely the iterative spread approximation and a more delicate counting argument, we improve the range of t in which this conjecture holds while maintaining the same stability result. 1 . Previous Work and Context 1 has seen significant progress over the past 50 years. The first progress towards proving 1 was due to Frankl and Deza [ FD77 ] , who proved 1 when n is sufficiently large in terms of n − t . Cameron and Ku, and Larose and Malvenuto [ CK03 , LM04 ] proved 1 for t = 1 . Their proof used Hoffman’s bound (upper bound on the size of an independent set of a graph) with weights chosen using representation theory of the symmetric group S n . For about ten years, there was no further improvement until Ellis and Lifshitz [ EL22 ] enhanced Ellis, Friedgut, and Pilpel’s result using Fourier-analytic techniques and showed that 1 holds for all t ≤ c ​ log ⁡ n log ⁡ log ⁡ n for some constant c > 0 . graphs with non-negative curvature have polynomial growth Guy Blachar Hervé Pajot Justin Salez Abstract A long-standing conjecture in the emerging discrete Bakry-Émery theory asserts that bounded-degree graphs satisfying CD ⁡ ( 0 , ∞ ) have polynomial growth. In the present paper, we prove this conjecture for all edge-regular graphs, and even obtain a volume doubling estimate with a constant that depends only on the degree. This is made possible thanks to the discovery of a surprising self-improvement phenomenon, which seems of independent interest: any edge-regular graph satisfying CD ⁡ ( κ , ∞ ) for some κ ∈ ℝ must in fact satisfy CD ⁡ ( κ , n ) for some explicit, universal and optimal dimension parameter n . 1 Introduction Let G = ( V , E ) be a locally finite, connected, simple, undirected graph, and let Δ denote its Laplacian operator, which acts on any function f ∈ ℝ V as follows: ∀ x ∈ V , Δ ​ f ​ ( x ) : ⁣ = ∑ y ∈ N x ( f ⁡ ( y ) − f ⁡ ( x ) ) , (1) where N x := { y ∈ V : { x , y } ∈ E } is the set of neighbors of x . If G has bounded degrees and satisfies CD ⁡ ( 0 , ∞ ) , then G has polynomial growth. More precisely, ∀ x ∈ V , ∀ r ∈ ℕ , # B ( x , r ) ≤ r D , (5) where the constant D < ∞ depends only on the maximum degree. The degree restriction is of course necessary here, since choosing r = 2 in ( 5 ) shows that D is at least logarithmic in the maximum degree. Conjecture 1 has received a lot of attention [ 8 , 18 , 21 ] , and several results have been establish in its direction [ 3 , 22 , 17 , 9 ] . In particular, a major breakthrough was recently obtained by Hutchcroft and Münch [ 9 ] , who proved that bounded-degree graphs with non-negative curvature in the (different) sense of Ollivier [ 19 ] have sub-exponential growth. Another remarkable result in the direction of Conjecture 1 is the fact that polynomial growth is indeed guaranteed when the assumption CD ⁡ ( 0 , ∞ ) is strengthened to its finite-dimensional refinement CD ⁡ ( 0 , n ) , for any n < ∞ . More precisely, the following Volume Doubling estimate was recently obtained by Münch [ 18 ] for finite graphs and by Pajot and Russ [ 21 ] in the infinite case (see also [ 3 ] for a closely related, wearker result). In the present paper, we establish Conjecture 1 for all edge-regular graphs. Let us here recall that G is edge-regular if there exist two integers d , τ ≥ 0 such that any vertex has exactly d neighbors, and any two adjacent vertices have exactly τ common neighbors: ∀ x ∈ V , # ​ N x = d , and ∀ { x , y } ∈ E , # ⁡ ( N x ∩ N y ) = τ . (7) For example, any triangle-free regular graph is edge-regular, as well as any Cayley graph generated by a conjugacy class. Our main contribution is the surprising discovery that an edge-regular graph satisfying CD ⁡ ( 0 , ∞ ) must actually satisfy the stronger property CD ⁡ ( 0 , d ) , where d is the degree. Our result is in fact substantially stronger: for any value of the curvature parameter κ ∈ ℝ , we determine the optimal value of the dimension parameter n such that CD ⁡ ( κ , ∞ ) is equivalent to CD ⁡ ( κ , n ) , simultaneously for all graphs satisfying ( 7 ). Theorem 2 (Self-improvement of the Bakry-Émery condition on edge-regular graphs) . Fix κ ∈ ℝ and two integers d , τ ≥ 0 , and suppose that G satisfies CD ⁡ ( κ , ∞ ) and ( 7 ). Then, 1. 2 ​ κ ≤ 4 + τ ; 2. CD ⁡ ( κ , n ) holds for n := 4 ​ d / ( 4 + τ − 2 ​ κ ) . 3. CD ⁡ ( κ , n ′ ) fails for any n ′ < n . In particular, any edge-regular graph satisfying CD ⁡ ( 0 , ∞ ) automatically satisfies CD ⁡ ( 0 , d ) where d is the degree, as promised. Any d − regular graph satisfying CD ⁡ ( 0 , ∞ ) satisfies CD ⁡ ( 0 , d ) . 2 Proof of the main result We will naturally say that the CD ⁡ ( κ , n ) condition holds at a vertex o ∈ V if Γ 2 ​ ( f , f ) ​ ( o ) ≥ κ ​ Γ 1 ​ ( f , f ) ​ ( o ) + ( Δ ​ f ​ ( o ) ) 2 n , (8) for all f ∈ ℝ V . Our proof of Theorem 2 relies on the construction of height functions . Definition 1 (Height functions) . Given two parameters κ , λ ∈ ℝ and a vertex o ∈ V , we say that g ∈ ℝ V is a ( κ , λ ) − height function at o if Δ ​ g ​ ( o ) = 1 and for all f ∈ ℝ V , Γ 2 ​ ( f , g ) ​ ( o ) = κ ​ Γ 1 ​ ( f , g ) ​ ( o ) + λ ​ Δ ​ f ​ ( o ) . The interest of this mysterious definition is contained in the following result, which shows that height functions systematically provide an optimal self-improvement in the local Bakry-Émery criterion. As the reader will see, the proof does not use the specific form of the discrete Laplacian ( 1 ): it applies to any Markov generator Δ on any state space. The converse part will not be used here, but it guarantees that there is no intrinsic limitation in using height functions as a general tool to establish curvature-dime

Let ν ⁡ ( G ) denote the matching number of G . A connected graph G is k -matching-critical if ν ⁡ ( G − S ) < ν ⁡ ( G ) for every S ⊆ V ⁡ ( G ) with | S | = k . A vertex v is a matching-critical vertex if ν ⁡ ( G − v ) < ν ⁡ ( G ) . Thus a matching-critical vertex is precisely a vertex covered by every maximum matching of G . The k -matching-critical condition is naturally expressed in terms of the matching deficiency def ⁡ ( G ) = | V ⁡ ( G ) | − 2 ​ ν ​ ( G ) , the number of vertices left uncovered by a maximum matching. aces of hypergraphs were initiated by Mubayi and Zhao [ 19 ] on the trace of 𝒦 t r . In the same paper, they proposed a conjecture of ex r ⁡ ( n , Tr r ⁡ ( 𝒦 t r ′ ) ) and proved the asymptotics for some cases. In this paper, we consider Tr r ⁡ ( ℱ ) where ℱ is a graph, denoted by F in the following. When F is a graph, Tr r ⁡ ( F ) is called graph-based hypergraphs. There are several ways to define graph-based hypergraphs. The first way is the expansion of a graph F , denoted by F ( r ) + , which is obtained from F by enlarging each edge of F with a set of r − 2 new vertices such that all these new sets are disjoint. Secondly, the Berge copies of a graph F , denoted by B r ​ ( F ) , is a family of r -uniform hypergraphs obtained by replacing each edge of F with a distinct r -edge containing it. Notice that F ( r ) + ∈ Tr r ⁡ ( F ) ⊆ B r ​ ( F ) . Thus we have ex r ⁡ ( n , B r ​ ( F ) ) ≤ ex r ⁡ ( n , Tr r ⁡ ( F ) ) ≤ ex r ⁡ ( n , F ( r ) + ) . The order of magnitude of ex r ​ ( n , Tr r ⁡ ( F ) ) was determined by Füredi and Luo [ 8 ] , in the following theorem. Theorem 1.1 (Füredi and Luo [ 8 ] ) . For any graph F with at least one edge, as n tends to infinity, we have ex r ​ ( n , Tr r ⁡ ( F ) ) = Θ ⁡ ( max 2 ≤ s ≤ r ⁡ { ex ⁡ ( n , K s , F ) } ) . There are several studies about ex r ⁡ ( n , Tr r ⁡ ( F ) ) for other graphs F . The study when F is an outerplanar graph or a star can be found in [ 8 , 20 ] . When r = 3 and F = K 2 , t , it is studied by Luo and Spiro [ 17 ] , and Qian and Ge [ 20 ] . When r = 3 and F = C 4 , it is studied by Luo and Spiro [ 17 ] , and Gerbner and Picollelli [ 9
 r − 1 ) ⁡ ( n − s ) + ( s r ) . For the Turán number of expansion of matching, Erdős [ 5 ] proposed the Erdős matching conjecture. It is known to hold for sufficiently large n . The most recent result is due to Frankl [ 6 ] , who proved that ex r ⁡ ( n , M s + 1 ( r ) + ) = ∑ i = 1 s ( s i ) ​ ( n − s r − i ) for r , s ≥ 1 and n ≥ ( 2 ​ s + 1 ) ​ r − s . In this paper, we study the Turán number of forbidding a matching M s + 1 as a trace. Before stating our main theorems, we need some definitions. Let ℋ be an r -uniform hypergraph. We say a set D of vertices is a dominating set of ℋ if for every vertex v ∉ D , there exists a hyperedge e such that v ∈ e and e ∖ { v } ⊆ D . The domination number of a hypergraph ℋ , denoted by γ ⁡ ( ℋ ) , is the minimum size of a dominating set in ℋ . There are many variants of domination in hypergraphs. Our version is introduced by Divya, Ramakrishnan, and Arumugam in [ 4 ] . We say a set S of vertices in an r -uniform hypergraph ℋ is a dominated set if its complement is a dominating set. The dominated number of a hypergraph ℋ , denoted by ϕ ⁡ ( ℋ ) , is the maximum size of a dominated set in ℋ . When r = 2 , the dominated set is also known as enclaveless set (see [ 22 ] ). The investigation of dominating sets in graphs can be found in [ 12 , 18 , 21 ] . By the definitions of dominating set and dominated set, we have ϕ ⁡ ( ℋ ) + γ ⁡ ( ℋ ) = v ⁡ ( ℋ ) . luding remarks To determine the coefficient f r − 1 ​ ( s ) in Theorem 1.2 is a natural question. We have the following conjecture. Conjecture 5.1 . f r ​ ( s ) achieves the maximum for either 𝒦 s + r − 1 r , or the hypergraph 𝒢 on s + r vertices obtained by removing a minimum edge set such that every ( r − 1 ) -set is covered at least once from the complete hypergraph 𝒦 s + r r . Especially, if the Steiner system S ⁡ ( s + r , r , r − 1 ) exists, then f r ​ ( s ) = ( s + r r ) − ( s + r r − 1 ) / r in this case (The Steiner system S ⁡ ( s + r , r , r − 1 ) is a set of r -element subsets of an ( s + r ) -element set such that each ( r − 1 ) -subset is contained in exactly one r -subset).
